    def test_decoder_accuracy(metrics, submitted_data_full, submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats):
        """Run the provided train_decoder.py script on the sample data and check that it runs without error."""
    
        train_params = {
            'npcs': 100,
            'lr': 1e-3,
            'l1_weight': 1e-4,
            'balanced_loss': True,
        }
    
        metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy'] = None
    
        if reference_data_stats is not None:
            ref_accuracy = reference_data_stats['validation_balanced_accuracy']
            metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy_reference'] = ref_accuracy
            metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy_ratio'] = None
    
        # train the decoder on the submitted data
        rng_state = reference_data_stats.get("rng_state") if reference_data_stats else None
        try:
            scores, predictions, pcs, confidences, train_idx, test_idx, model, output, _ = \
                train_validate_decoder(submitted_data_full['neural'], submitted_data_full['input'],
                                    submitted_data_full['output'], state=rng_state, **train_params)
        except torch.cuda.OutOfMemoryError:
            print("CUDA out of memory error during decoder training. Retrying on CPU...")
            train_params['device'] = torch.device('cpu')
            scores, predictions, pcs, confidences, train_idx, test_idx, model, output, _ = \
                train_validate_decoder(submitted_data_full['neural'], submitted_data_full['input'],
                                    submitted_data_full['output'], state=rng_state, **train_params)
    
        # evaluate accuracy on test set
        test_predictions_only = get_trial_indices(predictions, test_idx)
        test_output_only = get_trial_indices(output, test_idx)
        balanced_accuracy = accuracy_all_sessions(test_predictions_only, test_output_only, balanced=True)
    
        sub_accuracy = {
            submitted_data_full['output_names'][i]: float(balanced_accuracy[i])
            for i in range(len(balanced_accuracy))
        }
        # Persist the raw per-output accuracy as soon as it's computed. Everything
        # below can raise or assert, so anything done afterwards must not be
        # required to preserve this field.
        metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy'] = sub_accuracy
    
        if reference_data_stats is None:
            pytest.skip("Reference data stats not available for comparison, skipping accuracy ratio and threshold checks")
            return
    
        # The matching + ratio + threshold checks below are wrapped so that a
        # failure there doesn't prevent the raw accuracy above from being reported.
        # Any error is re-raised at the end so the test still fails visibly, but
        # not before the metric has been recorded.
        deferred_error = None
        try:
            # find a match between output dimensions (same as test_data_stats)
            output_matches, output_match_costs = match_outputs(
                submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats
            )
    
            metrics['output_match_cost'] = float(np.mean(output_match_costs))
    
            # Best-effort ratio: skip any match whose reference or submitted name
            # isn't resolvable (e.g. matcher returned inf cost / None).
            ratio = {}
            for ref_name, sub_name, cost in output_matches:
                if sub_name is None or sub_name not in sub_accuracy:
                    continue
                if ref_name is None or ref_name not in ref_accuracy or not ref_accuracy[ref_name]:
                    continue
                ratio[ref_name] = sub_accuracy[sub_name] / ref_accuracy[ref_name]
            metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy_ratio'] = ratio
    
            for ref_name, sub_name, _ in output_matches:
                if sub_name is None or sub_name not in sub_accuracy:
                    continue
                if ref_name is None or ref_name not in ref_accuracy:
                    continue
                ref_acc = ref_accuracy[ref_name]
                sub_acc = sub_accuracy[sub_name]
                assert sub_acc >= MIN_ACCURACY_FRAC * ref_acc, f'Accuracy for output {sub_name} ({sub_acc:.3f}) is below {MIN_ACCURACY_FRAC*100:.1f}% of reference accuracy ({ref_acc:.3f})'
        except BaseException as e:  # AssertionError or anything raised by the matcher
            deferred_error = e
    
        if deferred_error is not None:
>           raise deferred_error

It is in the file `datapaper.pdf`\n    16→  - A recent paper that uses this dataset is \"Brain-wide analysis reveals movement encoding structured across and within brain areas\". It is in the file `methodpaper.pdf`\n    17→  - A white paper about spike sorting and quality control is in the file `ChenLiuEtAl2023_SpikeSortingQC.pdf`\n    18→- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `methods.txt`. \n    19→- **Code**:\n    20→  - `code` contains code from `methodpaper.pdf`.\n    21→- **Data** from the papers are in the directory `data`.\n    22→\n    23→## Decoder Task\n    24→\n    25→- Temporally align based on **Go cue onset**. \n    26→- Extract **2.5 s before to 1.5 s after** the go cue for each trial\n    27→- Use **50-ms-width bins** for computing firing rates. \n    28→\n    29→### Decoder Inputs:\n    30→- Time from **tone onset** in seconds (continuous, time-varying)\n    31→- Whether **photostimulation** is on at every time point (discrete, time-varying)\n    32→\n    33→### Decoder Outputs\n    34→- Lick direction **choice** (left = 0, right = 1, per-trial)\n    35→- **Outcome** (ignore = 0, miss = 1, hit = 2, per-trial)\n    36→- **Early lick** (no = 0, yes = 1, per-trial)\n    37→- **Tongue y-position**, per-session discretization:\n    38→  - 0: < 40th percentile of y-position over the session\n    39→  - 1: 40th to 60th percentile of y-position over the session\n    40→  - 2: > 60th percentile of y-position over the session\n    41→\n    42→## Target Data Format\n    43→\n    44→- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `converted_data.pkl`.\n    45→- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n    46→\n    47→```python\n    48→data = {\n    49→    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n    50→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    51→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n    52→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n    53→            ...\n    54→        ],\n    55→        [  # list of trials for session 2\n    56→            ...\n    57→        ],\n    58→        ...\n    59→    ],\n    60→\n    61→    'input': [  # list of sessions\n    62→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    63→            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n    64→            ...\n    65→        ],\n    66→        ...\n    67→    ],\n    68→\n    69→    'output': [  # list of sessions\n    70→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    71→            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n    72→            ...\n    73→        ],\n    74→        ...\n    75→    ],\n    76→\n    77→    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    78→    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n    79→\n    80→    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    81→    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n    82→        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    83→    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n    84→\n    85→    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    86→    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    87→    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n    88→        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n    89→        ...\n    90→    ],\n    91→\n    92→    'metadata': {\n    93→        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n    94→        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n    95→        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n    96→        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n    97→        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n    98→        ...\n    99→    }\n   100→}\n   101→```\n   102→\n   103→### Key Format Requirements\n   104→\n   105→- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n   106→  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n   107→  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n   108→  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n   109→\n   110→- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n   111→  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n   112→  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n   113→    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n   114→    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n   115→    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n   116→  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n   117→  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n   118→  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n   119→\n   120→- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n   121→  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n   122→  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n   123→  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n   124→  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n   125→  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n   126→\n   127→- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n   128→- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n   129→  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n   130→\n   131→- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n   132→- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n   133→\n   134→- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n   135→  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n   136→\n   137→- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n   138→  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n   139→- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n   140→  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n   141→\n   142→- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n   143→  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n   144→  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n   145→  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n   146→  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n   147→  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n   148→\n   149→## Decoder Reference\n   150→\n   151→- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n   152→- Optional arguments:\n   153→  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n   154→  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n   155→  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n   156→- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n   157→\n   158→### Purpose\n   159→\n   160→These functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n   161→1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   162→2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   163→3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   164→4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n   165→5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n   166→\n   167→### Consistency requirements\n   168→\n   169→Your processing and formatting must **match the reference papers and code** with respect to:\n   170→- Loading of data\n   171→- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n   172→- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n   173→- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n   174→\n   175→To check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n   176→\n   177→Whenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n   178→\n   179→Discrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \n   180→\n   181→Carefully verify your work after every step. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code. \n   184→\n   185→## Success Criteria\n   186→\n   187→A successful conversion should:\n   188→1. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01DQTkPLSh2oE2S8sp7RZEM6","type":"tool_result","content":"     1→Behavior and video tracking\n     2→\n     3→Mice performed an auditory delayed response task 21 (Figure 1A). The instruction stimuli during the sample epoch were pure tones played at one of two frequencies (3 kHz or 12 kHz). Each tone was played three times for 150 ms with 100 ms inter-tone intervals. The sample epoch was followed by a 1.2 s delay epoch. An auditory ‘Go’ cue (carrier frequency of 6 kHz with 360 Hz modulating fre-\n     4→quency, 0.1 s duration) indicated the end of the delay epoch. Licking early during the sample/delay epoch triggered a replay of the epoch. During the response epoch (answer period: 1.5 s) mice reported the instruction by licking one of the two lick ports. Licking (consumption period: 1.5 s) the correct lick port triggered a small water reward (0.1 ~ 0.2 ul). Licking the incorrect lick port triggered a timeout (1-3 s). After mice stopped licking for 1.5 s, the trial ended, followed by a 250 ms inter-trial-interval. Early lick trials and no\n     5→response trials were excluded for analysis. Overall performance was computed as the fraction of correct control trials (i.e. no photostimulation), excluding any early lick trials. We selected experimental sessions for analysis based on following criteria: overall behavioral performance (> 65%), and at least 50 correct lick left and lick right trials each. Mice performed on average 476 (Mean; range, 130-785) trials per session with 84% correct rate (range, 65-99%; STAR Methods). During the delay epoch, mice maintain a memory of their choice and plan a movement.\n     6→\n     7→Two CMOS cameras (CM3-U3-13Y3M, FLIR) were used to track orofacial movements of the mouse under IR light illumination (940 nm LED). The cameras were equipped with 4-12 mm focal length lenses (12VM412ASIR, Tamron) and a pixel resolution of 71 mm. High-speed videos from a side view and a bottom view (Figure 1C) were acquired at 300 Hz using software FlyCapture (TELEDYNE FLIR). We trained DeepLabCut 39 to track the movement of tongue, jaw and nose (Figure 1I).\n     8→\n     9→Multi-regional electrophysiological recordings\n    10→\n    11→We designed a ﬂexible manipulator system for multiple Neuropixels probe insertions (https://www.janelia.org/open-science/manipulator-system-for-multiple-neuropixels-probe-recordings). Three to four small craniotomies (diameter, 1 \u0018 1.5 mm) were made over target brain areas one day before the ﬁrst recording session. Details of recordings made with multiple Neuropixels probes in head-restrained behaving animals have been published (dx.doi.org/10.17504/protocols.io.8tphwmn). Target regions, insertions angles, probe types, etc. are summarized in Table S2. All coordinates are given with respect to Bregma (AP, anterior-posterior; ML, medial-lateral; DV, dorsal-ventral).\n    12→\n    13→Extracellular spikes were recorded using Neuropixels 1.0 probes, 2.0 single-shank (SS) and 2.0 multi-shank (MS) probes 3,4 (Table S2). Three to seven recordings were made from each craniotomy. Recording depth was inferred from manipulator readings (Sensapax uMp manipulators). After completion of probe insertion, brain tissue was allowed to settle for several minutes before recording. During recordings the craniotomies were immersed in saline.\n    14→\n    15→Probes were conﬁgured and data were visualized and streamed to disk using the open-source software package SpikeGLX (https://billkarsh.github.io/SpikeGLX/). Data from up to ﬁve simultaneously recorded probes were acquired at 30 kHz. Sync waves (0.5 s duty cycle TTL pulses) were recorded across probes and on auxiliary channels for synchronization.\n    16→\n    17→Photoinhibition\n    18→\n    19→Light from two 473 nm lasers (Coherent OBIS LS/LX) was used to illuminate the two hemispheres of ALM. The laser power was controlled by analog outputs controlled by a Bpod (Sanworks). Photoinactivation of ALM (centered on AP 2.5 mm; ML 1.5 mm) was performed through clear-skull cap implant11 or via craniotomy by directing the laser over the skull (beam diameter, 1.8 mm at 4 sigma). Photoinhibition of the left-, right-ALM hemisphere, or both hemispheres was deployed on a subset of ~25% randomly interleaved trials 11,21 (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice). To prevent the mice from distinguishing photostimulation trials from control rials using visual cues, a ‘masking ﬂash’ (1 ms pulses at 10 Hz) was delivered using 470 nm LEDs in front of the mice throughout the entire trial. For silencing ALM, we stimulated cortical GABAergic neurons in VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice. We used 40 Hz photostimulation with a sinusoidal temporal proﬁle (5 mW average power per hemisphere) and a 100 ms linear ramping down during the laser offset to reduce rebound neuronal activity. This photo stimulus is expected to silence glutamatergic neurons in all of ALM. 37 We silenced ALM activity during the late delay epoch (last 0.5 s), including the 100 ms ramp-down. Thus, photoinhibition always ended before the ‘Go’ cue.\n    20→\n    21→To analyze the effects of photoinhibition, units with at least 10 photostimulation trials were selected for analysis (Figures 5 and S5). Bilateral ALM photostimulation reduced behavior performance from 83.2% to 71.7% (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice, n = 93 sessions). To exclude the effects of rebound population activity in the thalamus 16 and other subcortical regions, we used a 20-120 ms\n    22→time window after photo stimulation onset to measure the effect of ALM inactivation in downstream regions (Figures 5 and S5). For ALM itself, we used 500 ms (i.e. the entire inactivation period) to calculate spike rate reduction.\n    23→\n    24→Spike sorting and quality control\n    25→\n    26→We developed a data pipeline for preprocessing, spike sorting and quality control (https://github.com/jenniferColonell/ecephys_spike_sorting). The extracellular recording traces were ﬁrst band-pass ﬁltered (Butterworth acausal ﬁlter, 250 Hz - 9 kHz). We then sorted the dataset using both Kilosort2 32 (MATLAB version, GitHub - jenniferColonell/KS20_for_preprocessed_data: Release version of KS2 for preprocessed data) and Kilosort2.5 4 (Python version, GitHub - jenniferColonell/pykilosort: [WIP] Python port of Kilosort 2). Both sorting methods gave similar results. In the analyses presented in the paper, we used the output from Kilosort2. Each output cluster was associated with a list of 15 quality metrics calculated to assess isolation and sorting quality 7 (i.e. quality control) (https://github.com/AllenInstitute/ecephys_spike_sorting/tree/master/ecephys_spike_sorting/modules/quality_metrics).\n    27→\n    28→The details of the spike sorting and quality control are described in an accompanying white paper (https://doi.org/10.25378/janelia.24066108.v1). In brief, we used 15 cluster quality metrics to train classiﬁers - one for each of the major ﬁve brain areas (cortex, striatum, thalamus, midbrain and medulla). The classiﬁers were trained based on labels derived from manual curation. We curated Kilosort2 output (28 penetrations from ﬁve brain areas) using the open-source graphical user interface Phy (GitHub - cortex-lab/phy: phy: interactive visualization and manual spike sorting of large-scale ephys data). Each cluster was labeled as a ‘good’ or ‘unlabeled’ unit. Annotators were blind to location, recording condition and trial type information. Based on the annotated labels of all clusters and associated quality metrics, we trained ﬁve region-speciﬁc classiﬁers using logistic regression. Applying the trained classiﬁers to the rest of the dataset provided lists of units that were labeled as ‘good’ and were used for the analysis in this paper. We applied the cortex classiﬁer to ALM and other cortex, hippocampus, olfactory and cortical subplate units; the striatum classiﬁer to striatum and pallidum units; the thalamus classiﬁer to thalamus and hypothalamus units; the midbrain classiﬁer to midbrain and pons units; the medulla classiﬁer to medulla and cerebellum units. We calculated the rate at which the classiﬁer incorrectly labeled ’unlabeled’ units as ’good’, using 10-fold cross-validation on held-out manually labeled units (false alarm rate): 7.8%; striatum: 6.4%; thalamus: 7.3%; midbrain: 5.5%; medulla 4.3%. This method yielded 8717 good units from ALM, 7664 from striatum, 12808 from thalamus, 7495 from midbrain and 2928 from medulla, etc. Overall, the dataset consisted of 69,943 good units recorded across 173 behavioral sessions, from which 655 probe insertions were made. This corresponds to 25.9 % of clusters reported by Kilosort2. \n    29→"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"6a6112d3-e9c0-45b7-9b75-a07b6e3fa4ae","uuid":"35fedcfe-8985-461d-8b1d-028626a4750a","timestamp":"2026-07-31T01:39:04.410Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/app/methods.txt","content":"Behavior and video tracking\n\nMice performed an auditory delayed response task 21 (Figure 1A). The instruction stimuli during the sample epoch were pure tones played at one of two frequencies (3 kHz or 12 kHz). Each tone was played three times for 150 ms with 100 ms inter-tone intervals. The sample epoch was followed by a 1.2 s delay epoch. An auditory ‘Go’ cue (carrier frequency of 6 kHz with 360 Hz modulating fre-\nquency, 0.1 s duration) indicated the end of the delay epoch. Licking early during the sample/delay epoch triggered a replay of the epoch. During the response epoch (answer period: 1.5 s) mice reported the instruction by licking one of the two lick ports. Licking (consumption period: 1.5 s) the correct lick port triggered a small water reward (0.1 ~ 0.2 ul). Licking the incorrect lick port triggered a timeout (1-3 s). After mice stopped licking for 1.5 s, the trial ended, followed by a 250 ms inter-trial-interval. Early lick trials and no\nresponse trials were excluded for analysis. Overall performance was computed as the fraction of correct control trials (i.e. no photostimulation), excluding any early lick trials. We selected experimental sessions for analysis based on following criteria: overall behavioral performance (> 65%), and at least 50 correct lick left and lick right trials each. Mice performed on average 476 (Mean; range, 130-785) trials per session with 84% correct rate (range, 65-99%; STAR Methods). During the delay epoch, mice maintain a memory of their choice and plan a movement.\n\nTwo CMOS cameras (CM3-U3-13Y3M, FLIR) were used to track orofacial movements of the mouse under IR light illumination (940 nm LED). The cameras were equipped with 4-12 mm focal length lenses (12VM412ASIR, Tamron) and a pixel resolution of 71 mm. High-speed videos from a side view and a bottom view (Figure 1C) were acquired at 300 Hz using software FlyCapture (TELEDYNE FLIR). We trained DeepLabCut 39 to track the movement of tongue, jaw and nose (Figure 1I).\n\nMulti-regional electrophysiological recordings\n\nWe designed a ﬂexible manipulator system for multiple Neuropixels probe insertions (https://www.janelia.org/open-science/manipulator-system-for-multiple-neuropixels-probe-recordings). Three to four small craniotomies (diameter, 1 \u0018 1.5 mm) were made over target brain areas one day before the ﬁrst recording session. Details of recordings made with multiple Neuropixels probes in head-restrained behaving animals have been published (dx.doi.org/10.17504/protocols.io.8tphwmn). Target regions, insertions angles, probe types, etc. are summarized in Table S2. All coordinates are given with respect to Bregma (AP, anterior-posterior; ML, medial-lateral; DV, dorsal-ventral).\n\nExtracellular spikes were recorded using Neuropixels 1.0 probes, 2.0 single-shank (SS) and 2.0 multi-shank (MS) probes 3,4 (Table S2). Three to seven recordings were made from each craniotomy. Recording depth was inferred from manipulator readings (Sensapax uMp manipulators). After completion of probe insertion, brain tissue was allowed to settle for several minutes before recording. During recordings the craniotomies were immersed in saline.\n\nProbes were conﬁgured and data were visualized and streamed to disk using the open-source software package SpikeGLX (https://billkarsh.github.io/SpikeGLX/). Data from up to ﬁve simultaneously recorded probes were acquired at 30 kHz. Sync waves (0.5 s duty cycle TTL pulses) were recorded across probes and on auxiliary channels for synchronization.\n\nPhotoinhibition\n\nLight from two 473 nm lasers (Coherent OBIS LS/LX) was used to illuminate the two hemispheres of ALM. The laser power was controlled by analog outputs controlled by a Bpod (Sanworks). Photoinactivation of ALM (centered on AP 2.5 mm; ML 1.5 mm) was performed through clear-skull cap implant11 or via craniotomy by directing the laser over the skull (beam diameter, 1.8 mm at 4 sigma). Photoinhibition of the left-, right-ALM hemisphere, or both hemispheres was deployed on a subset of ~25% randomly interleaved trials 11,21 (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice). To prevent the mice from distinguishing photostimulation trials from control rials using visual cues, a ‘masking ﬂash’ (1 ms pulses at 10 Hz) was delivered using 470 nm LEDs in front of the mice throughout the entire trial. For silencing ALM, we stimulated cortical GABAergic neurons in VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice. We used 40 Hz photostimulation with a sinusoidal temporal proﬁle (5 mW average power per hemisphere) and a 100 ms linear ramping down during the laser offset to reduce rebound neuronal activity. This photo stimulus is expected to silence glutamatergic neurons in all of ALM. 37 We silenced ALM activity during the late delay epoch (last 0.5 s), including the 100 ms ramp-down. Thus, photoinhibition always ended before the ‘Go’ cue.\n\nTo analyze the effects of photoinhibition, units with at least 10 photostimulation trials were selected for analysis (Figures 5 and S5). Bilateral ALM photostimulation reduced behavior performance from 83.2% to 71.7% (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice, n = 93 sessions). To exclude the effects of rebound population activity in the thalamus 16 and other subcortical regions, we used a 20-120 ms\ntime window after photo stimulation onset to measure the effect of ALM inactivation in downstream regions (Figures 5 and S5). For ALM itself, we used 500 ms (i.e. the entire inactivation period) to calculate spike rate reduction.\n\nSpike sorting and quality control\n\nWe developed a data pipeline for preprocessing, spike sorting and quality control (https://github.com/jenniferColonell/ecephys_spike_sorting). The extracellular recording traces were ﬁrst band-pass ﬁltered (Butterworth acausal ﬁlter, 250 Hz - 9 kHz). We then sorted the dataset using both Kilosort2 32 (MATLAB version, GitHub - jenniferColonell/KS20_for_preprocessed_data: Release version of KS2 for preprocessed data) and Kilosort2.5 4 (Python version, GitHub - jenniferColonell/pykilosort: [WIP] Python port of Kilosort 2). Both sorting methods gave similar results. In the analyses presented in the paper, we used the output from Kilosort2. Each output cluster was associated with a list of 15 quality metrics calculated to assess isolation and sorting quality 7 (i.e. quality control) (https://github.com/AllenInstitute/ecephys_spike_sorting/tree/master/ecephys_spike_sorting/modules/quality_metrics).\n\nThe details of the spike sorting and quality control are described in an accompanying white paper (https://doi.org/10.25378/janelia.24066108.v1). In brief, we used 15 cluster quality metrics to train classiﬁers - one for each of the major ﬁve brain areas (cortex, striatum, thalamus, midbrain and medulla). The classiﬁers were trained based on labels derived from manual curation. We curated Kilosort2 output (28 penetrations from ﬁve brain areas) using the open-source graphical user interface Phy (GitHub - cortex-lab/phy: phy: interactive visualization and manual spike sorting of large-scale ephys data). Each cluster was labeled as a ‘good’ or ‘unlabeled’ unit. Annotators were blind to location, recording condition and trial type information. Based on the annotated labels of all clusters and associated quality metrics, we trained ﬁve region-speciﬁc classiﬁers using logistic regression. Applying the trained classiﬁers to the rest of the dataset provided lists of units that were labeled as ‘good’ and were used for the analysis in this paper. We applied the cortex classiﬁer to ALM and other cortex, hippocampus, olfactory and cortical subplate units; the striatum classiﬁer to striatum and pallidum units; the thalamus classiﬁer to thalamus and hypothalamus units; the midbrain classiﬁer to midbrain and pons units; the medulla classiﬁer to medulla and cerebellum units. This method yielded 8717 good units from ALM, 7664 from striatum, 12808 from thalamus, 7495 from midbrain and 2928 from medulla, etc. Overall, the dataset consisted of 69,943 good units recorded across 173 behavioral sessions, from which 655 probe insertions were made. This is to deal with cases where for e.g. (bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride is actually 43 but evaluates to 42.999999... due to numerical errors in floating-point arithmetic.\n    45→    if np.allclose((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride, math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+1):\n    46→        n_bins =  math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+2\n    47→    else:\n    48→        n_bins = math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+1\n    49→    binCenters = bin_begin_time + np.arange(n_bins)*stride\n    50→\n    51→    binIntervals = np.vstack((binCenters-bin_width/2, binCenters+bin_width/2)).T\n    52→\n    53→    # Count number of spikes for each sliding bin\n    54→    binSpikes = np.asarray([[[np.sum(np.all([trial>=binInt[0], trial<binInt[1]], axis=0)) for binInt in binIntervals] for trial in unit]\n    55→        for unit in spikeTimes]).swapaxes(0,-1)\n    56→\n    57→    if rate:\n    58→        return binCenters, binSpikes/float(bin_width) # Values are list of good neuron IDs (in MATLAB it starts from 1 but here we change it to start from 0).\n   170→            for area, id_list in qc_file['Idx'].items():\n   171→                if id_list == []:\n   172→                    idx_qc_dict[area] = []\n   173→                elif isinstance(id_list, int) or isinstance(id_list, float): # only one neuron\n   174→                    idx_qc_dict[area] = [id_list-1]\n   175→                else:\n   176→                    idx_qc_dict[area] = list(np.array(id_list) - 1)\n   177→\n   178→            anno_qc_dict = {}\n   179→            for name, value in qc_file['AnnoName'].items():\n   180→                if isinstance(value, str):\n   181→                    anno_qc_dict[name] = [value]\n   182→                else:\n   183→                    anno_qc_dict[name] = value\n   184→\n   185→        # --- 1.2 neural data ---\n   186→        # -- 1.2.1 spike times\n   187→        spike_times = sess_file['neuron_single_units'] # ndarray. spike_times[neuron_id][trial_id] is ndarray\n   188→\n   189→        # -- 1.2.2 unit_info and QC stats\n   190→        unit_info = sess_file['neuron_unit_info'] # ndarray (n_units, 8)\n   191→        # neuron_unit_info: columns: unit_id, unit_quality, unit_x_in_um, depth_in_um, associated_electrode, shank, cell_type, recording_location. For putative type, we have: 'Pyr' - pyramidal neurons, 'FS' - fast spiking, 'not classified', 'all' - all types\n   192→        probe_no_col = probe * np.ones((unit_info.shape[0], 1)) # (n_units,1) original probe #\n   193→        unit_info = np.concatenate([unit_info, probe_no_col], axis=1) # (n_units, 9)\n   194→        unit_qc = sess_file['neuron_unit_quality_control']\n   195→        # dictionary. Needed keys: 'unit_amp', 'presence_ratio', 'amplitude_cutoff', 'isi_violation', 'unit_snr', 'avg_firing_rate', 'drift_metric'. Values are all list with length n_neurons.\n   196→\n   197→        # -- 1.2.3 histology\n   198→        histology = sess_file['histology'] # dictionary, keys: 'unit', 'ccf_x', 'ccf_y', 'ccf_z', 'annotation'\n   199→        ccf_unit_id = np.array(histology['unit']) # ndarray, (n_neurons,)\n   200→        ccf_coordinate = np.stack([histology['ccf_x'], histology['ccf_y'], histology['ccf_z']]).swapaxes(0,1) # ndarray (n_neurons,3), xyz coordinates\n   201→        ccf_label = histology['annotation'] # list of np.str_\n   202→        ccf_label = [str(x).strip() for x in ccf_label] # list of string, with both whitespace at the beginning and end of each string removed\n   203→\n   204→        # -- 1.2.4 get units that have both ephys and histology\n   205→        unit_comb = set(unit_info[:,0]) & set(ccf_unit_id) # get the id of units that have both ephys and histology\n   206→        mask_ephys = [True if x in unit_comb else False for x in unit_info[:,0]] # mask for all other data (ephys) except histology\n   207→        mask_histology =[True if x in unit_comb else False for x in ccf_unit_id] # mask for histology only\n   208→\n   209→        # mask CCF info\n   210→        ccf_coordinate = ccf_coordinate[mask_histology,:] # ndarray (n_neurons,3), xyz coordinates\n   211→        ccf_label = list(compress(ccf_label, mask_histology)) # list of string, with both whitespace at the beginning and end of each string removed\n   212→        ccf_unit_id = ccf_unit_id[mask_histology]\n   213→\n   214→        # mask ephys info\n   215→        unit_info = unit_info[mask_ephys,:] # (n_neurons, 9)\n   216→        spike_times = spike_times[mask_ephys]\n   217→        for name in unit_qc.keys():\n   218→            unit_qc[name] = list(np.array(unit_qc[name])[mask_ephys])\n   219→\n   220→        # --- 1.3 add probe data to sess data ---\n   221→        spike_times_sess.append(spike_times) # ndarray. spike_times[neuron_id][trial_id] is ndarray\n   222→        unit_info_sess.append(unit_info) # (n_units, 9)\n   223→        unit_qc_sess_list.append(unit_qc) # dictionary. Needed keys: 'unit_amp', 'presence_ratio', 'amplitude_cutoff', 'isi_violation', 'unit_snr', 'avg_firing_rate', 'drift_metric'. Values are all list with length n_neurons.\n   224→        ccf_coordinate_sess.append(ccf_coordinate) # ndarray (n_neurons,3), xyz coordinates\n   225→        ccf_label_sess = ccf_label_sess + ccf_label # list\n   226→        ccf_unit_id_sess.append(ccf_unit_id) # ndarray, (n_neurons,)\n   227→\n   228→    # ----- 2. combine data from all probes ---\n   229→    spike_times_sess = np.concatenate(spike_times_sess) # ndarray. spike_times[neuron_id][trial_id] is ndarray\n   230→    unit_info_sess = np.concatenate(unit_info_sess, axis=0) # (n_units, 9)\n   231→\n   232→    # uncommented\n   233→    unit_qc_sess = {'unit_amp': [], 'presence_ratio': [], 'amplitude_cutoff': [], 'isi_violation': [], 'avg_firing_rate':[], 'drift_metric': []}\n   234→    for probe_qc_dict in unit_qc_sess_list:\n   235→        for qc_name in unit_qc_sess.keys():\n   236→            unit_qc_sess[qc_name] = unit_qc_sess[qc_name] + probe_qc_dict[qc_name]\n   237→\n   238→    ccf_coordinate_sess = np.concatenate(ccf_coordinate_sess, axis=0) # ndarray (n_neurons,3), xyz coordinates\n   239→    ccf_unit_id_sess = np.concatenate(ccf_unit_id_sess) # ndarray, (n_neurons,)\n   240→\n   241→    n_neurons = len(spike_times_sess)\n   242→    assert unit_info_sess.shape[0] == n_neurons\n   243→    assert len(unit_qc_sess['drift_metric']) == n_neurons\n   244→    assert ccf_coordinate_sess.shape[0] == n_neurons\n   245→    assert len(ccf_label_sess) == n_neurons\n   246→    assert len(ccf_unit_id_sess) == n_neurons\n   247→    # print('total neurons:', n_neurons)\n   248→\n   249→    # ----- 3. Process neurons from areas of interest and perform QC filtering -----\n   250→    i_file = 1\n   251→    neuron_id_used = []\n   252→    neuron_id_all = np.arange(n_neurons) # neuron_id is continuous from 0. unit_id is the original id from DJ.\n   253→\n   254→    for region in ['ALM', 'Medulla', 'Midbrain', 'Striatum', 'Thalamus', 'Pons', 'Cerebellum', 'Hypothalamus', 'Hippocampus', 'Orbital', 'OtherCortex', 'Olfactory', 'CorticalSubplate', 'Pallidum']:\n   255→        for side in ['left', 'right']:\n   256→            area = '_'.join([side, region])\n   257→            area_key = '{}_qc'.format(region)\n   258→            neuron_id_area = helper_get_neuron_id_area(idx_qc_dict[area_key], anno_qc_dict[area_key], ccf_coordinate_sess, ccf_label_sess, side, region)\n   259→\n   260→            if len(neuron_id_area) == 0:\n   261→                continue\n   262→\n   263→            print('Filtered neurons:', area, len(neuron_id_area))\n   264→\n   265→            # record used neurons\n   266→            neuron_id_used = neuron_id_used + neuron_id_area\n   267→\n   268→            save_flag = process_one_area(sess_name, area, neuron_id_area, i_file, bw, stride, begin_time, end_time, save_folder_sess, spike_times_sess, unit_info_sess, unit_qc_sess, ccf_coordinate_sess, ccf_label_sess, ccf_unit_id_sess, sess_dict)\n   269→\n   270→            if save_flag:\n   271→                i_file += 1\n   272→\n   273→    # ----- 4. Don't process the rest of the neurons -----\n   274→    with open(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, 'others_finished.txt'), 'w') as f:\n   275→        f.write('{}: finished.'.format(sess_name))\n   276→\n   277→    '''\n   278→    neuron_id_others = set(neuron_id_all) - set(neuron_id_used)\n   279→    neuron_id_others = sorted(list(neuron_id_others))\n   280→\n   281→    if len(neuron_id_others)==0:\n   282→        return\n   283→\n   284→    print('Filtered neurons:', 'others', len(neuron_id_others))\n   285→    process_one_area(sess_name, 'others', neuron_id_others, i_file, bw, stride, begin_time, end_time, save_folder_sess, spike_times_sess, unit_info_sess, ccf_coordinate_sess, ccf_label_sess, ccf_unit_id_sess, sess_dict)\n   286→    '''\n   287→\n   288→\n   289→def process_one_area(sess_name, area, neuron_id_area, i_file, bw, stride, begin_time, end_time, save_folder_sess, spike_times_sess, unit_info_sess, unit_qc_sess, ccf_coordinate_sess, ccf_label_sess, ccf_unit_id_sess, sess_dict):\n   290→    \"\"\"\n   291→    Get all neurons in an area, extract firing rates, and then save data.\n   292→    \"\"\"\n   293→    if os.path.exists(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, '%d_%s.pickle' % (i_file, area))):\n   294→        return True\n   295→\n   296→    # filter using the neuron mask\n   297→    n_units = unit_info_sess.shape[0]\n   298→    spike_times_area = spike_times_sess[neuron_id_area]\n   299→    unit_info_area = unit_info_sess[neuron_id_area,:] # (n_units, 9)\n   300→\n   301→    # uncommented\n   302→    unit_qc_area = {}\n   303→    for qc_name in unit_qc_sess.keys():\n   304→        unit_qc_area[qc_name] = np.array(unit_qc_sess[qc_name])[neuron_id_area]\n   305→        assert len(unit_qc_sess[qc_name]) == n_units\n   306→\n   307→    ccf_coordinate_area = ccf_coordinate_sess[neuron_id_area,:] # ndarray (n_neurons,3)\n   308→    ccf_label_area = list(np.array(ccf_label_sess)[neuron_id_area])\n   309→    ccf_unit_id_area = list(ccf_unit_id_sess[neuron_id_area])\n   310→\n   311→    # truncate spike times into desired time window (already aligned to go cue in raw data)\n   312→    for i_cluster, st_cluster in enumerate(spike_times_area):\n   313→        for i_trial, st_trial in enumerate(st_cluster):\n   314→            # if spike_times[i_cluster][i_trial] != []:\n   315→            #     amin_all.append(np.amin(spike_times[i_cluster][i_trial]))\n   316→            #     amax_all.append(np.amax(spike_times[i_cluster][i_trial]))\n   317→            if isinstance(st_trial, float) or isinstance(st_trial, int):\n   318→                spike_times_area[i_cluster][i_trial] = np.array([st_trial])\n   319→            # temp = spike_times[i_cluster][i_trial].copy()\n   320→            temp = spike_times_area[i_cluster][i_trial] # in Susu's data, spike_times are relative to go cue time\n   321→            spike_times_area[i_cluster][i_trial] = spike_times_area[i_cluster][i_trial][(temp>=begin_time)*(temp<=end_time)]\n   322→\n   323→    # bin spike times into firing rates\n   324→    bin_centers, fr = sliding_histogram(spike_times_area, begin_time, end_time, bin_width=bw, stride=stride, rate=True) # fr: (n_bins, n_trials, n_neurons)\n   325→\n   326→    if os.path.exists(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, '%d_%s.pickle' % (i_file, area))):\n   327→        with open(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, '%d_%s.pickle' % (i_file, area)), 'rb') as f: # HERE\n   328→            area_dict_exist = pickle.load(f)\n   329→            fr = area_dict_exist['fr']\n   330→            spike_times_area = area_dict_exist['spike_times']\n   331→            bin_centers = area_dict_exist['bin_centers']\n   332→\n   333→    # organize data into dict\n   334→    area_dict = {}\n   335→    area_dict['sess_name'] = sess_name # str\n   336→    area_dict['area'] = area # str\n   337→\n   338→    area_dict['bin_centers'] = bin_centers\n   339→    area_dict['fr'] = fr # (n_bins, n_trials, n_neurons)\n   340→    area_dict['spike_times'] = spike_times_area # ndarray. spike_times[neuron_id][trial_id] is ndarray with all spike times of this neuron in this trial. (go cue time is 0.)\n   341→    area_dict['neuron_info'] = unit_info_area # ndarray (n_units, 9)\n   342→    #  columns: unit_id, unit_quality, unit_x_in_um, depth_in_um, associated_electrode, shank, cell_type, recording_location, probe #. For putative type, we have: 'Pyr' - pyramidal neurons, 'FS' - fast spiking, 'not classified', 'all' - all types\n   343→    area_dict['unit_qc'] = unit_qc_area # dict. Keys are 'unit_amp', 'presence_ratio', 'amplitude_cutoff', 'isi_violation',, 'avg_firing_rate', 'drift_metric'. Values are all list with length n_neurons.\n   344→    area_dict['ccf_coordinate'] = ccf_coordinate_area # ndarray (n_neurons,3), xyz coordinates\n   345→    area_dict['ccf_label'] = ccf_label_area # list of string, with whitespace at the beginning and end of each string removed\n   346→    area_dict['ccf_unit_id'] = ccf_unit_id_area # ndarray (n_neurons,), id of units with histology\n   347→\n   348→    for name, info in sess_dict.items():\n   349→        area_dict[name] = info\n   350→\n   351→    # save data\n   352→    with open(os.path.join(save_folder_sess, '%d_%s.pickle' % (i_file, area)), 'wb') as f:\n   353→        pickle.dump(area_dict, f)\n   354→\n   355→    return True\n   356→\n   357→\n   358→\n   359→def helper_get_neuron_id_area(neuron_idx_list, anno_qc_list, ccf_coor, ccf_label, side, region):\n   360→    \"\"\"\n   361→    Filter the neurons in data_sess so that only neurons in side_region is left.\n   362→\n   363→    Args:\n   364→        neuron_idx_list (list): list of good neuron IDs.\n   365→        anno_qc_list (list): list of CCF annotations of good neurons.\n   366→        ccf_coor (ndarray): ndarray (n_neurons,3), xyz coordinates.\n   367→        ccf_label (list): list of string (CCF annotations), with length n_neurons.\n   368→        side (str): 'left' or 'right'.\n   369→        region (str): 'ALM', 'Medulla', 'Midbrain', 'Striatum', 'Thalamus', 'BLA', or 'ECT'.\n   370→\n   371→    Returns:\n   372→        neuron_id_area (list): id of neurons within side_region.\n   373→    \"\"\"\n   374→    if len(neuron_idx_list) == 0:\n   375→        return []\n   376→\n   377→    n_neurons = ccf_coor.shape[0]\n   378→    assert len(ccf_label) == n_neurons\n   379→    neurons_all = np.arange(n_neurons)\n   380→\n   381→    # filter neurons using side\n   382→    ML_mid_coor = 5700\n   383→    if side == 'left':\n   384→        mask_side = ccf_coor[:,0] >= ML_mid_coor\n   385→    elif side == 'right':\n   386→        mask_side = ccf_coor[:,0] < ML_mid_coor\n   387→    neuron_id_side = neurons_all[mask_side]\n   388→\n   389→    if len(neuron_id_side) == 0:\n   390→        return []\n   391→\n   392→    # combine side filter with qc filter\n   393→    neuron_id_area = sorted(list(set(neuron_id_side) & set(neuron_idx_list)))\n   394→    neuron_id_area = [idx for idx in neuron_id_area if ccf_label[idx] != []]\n   395→    if len(neuron_id_area) == 0:\n   396→        return []\n   397→    ccf_label_list = list(np.array(ccf_label)[neuron_id_area])\n   398→    ccf_label_joined = ''.join(ccf_label_list)\n   399→\n   400→    idx_side = [i for i, idx in enumerate(neuron_idx_list) if idx in neuron_id_area]\n   401→    anno_qc_list = list(np.array(anno_qc_list)[idx_side])\n   402→    anno_qc_joined = ''.join(anno_qc_list)\n   403→\n   404→    # temp = list(np.array(ccf_label)[neuron_id_area])\n   405→    # print(len(temp), len(anno_qc_list))\n   406→    # num = np.amin([len(temp), len(anno_qc_list)])\n   407→    # for ii in range(num):\n   408→        # if temp[ii] != anno_qc_list[ii]:\n   409→        # print(ii, temp[ii], anno_qc_list[ii])\n   410→    assert ccf_label_joined == anno_qc_joined\n   411→\n   412→    '''\n   413→    # filter neurons using region\n   414→    if region == 'ALM':\n   415→        res = 100 # resolution: 100 um\n   416→        # ALM_voxels = np.load(open('ALM_voxels_raw.npy', 'rb')) # (n_voxels,3), xyz of ALM\n   417→        ALM_voxels = np.load(open('ALM_voxels_symmetric.npy', 'rb')) # (n_voxels,3), xyz of ALM regions\n   418→\n   419→        diff = ccf_coor[:,None,:] - ALM_voxels[None,:,:] # (n_neurons, n_voxels, 3)\n   420→        mask_region = (np.abs(diff[:,:,0]) <= res/2) * (diff[:,:,1] <= res/2) * (np.abs(diff[:,:,2]) <= res/2) # (n_neurons, n_voxels)\n   421→        mask_region = np.any(mask_region, axis=1) # (n_neurons,)\n   422→\n   423→        neuron_id_region = neurons_all[mask_region]\n   424→    else:\n   425→        mask_region, neuron_id_region = get_id_subarea(region, ccf_label)\n   426→    '''\n   427→\n   428→    # neuron_id_area = sorted(list( set(neuron_id_side) & set(neuron_id_region)))\n   429→    return neuron_id_area\n   430→\n   431→\n   432→def helper_filter_by_neuron_id(data_sess, neuron_id_area, probe_no, area_name):\n   433→    \"\"\"\n   434→    Using the given neuron_id_area, filter the neuron sin data_sess.\n   435→\n   436→    Arg:\n   437→        data_sess (dict): same format as sess_dict.\n   438→        neuron_id_area (list): list of neuron if to use.\n   439→        probe_no (int): probe number.\n   440→        area_name (str):  name of the area. e.g. 'left_ALM'.\n   441→\n   442→    Returns:\n   443→        data_area (dict): same format as sess_dict.\n   444→    \"\"\"\n   445→    data_area = {}\n   446→\n   447→    data_area['mouse'] = data_sess['mouse']\n   448→    data_area['date'] = data_sess['date']\n   449→    data_area['probe'] = probe_no\n   450→    data_area['area'] = area_name\n   451→\n   452→    data_area['bin_centers'] = data_sess['bin_centers']\n   453→    data_area['fr'] = data_sess['fr'][:,:,neuron_id_area] # (n_bins, n_trials, n_neurons)\n   454→    data_area['spike_times'] = data_sess['spike_times'][neuron_id_area] # ndarray. spike_times[neuron_id][trial_id] is ndarray with all spike times of this neuron in this trial. (go cue time is 0.)\n   455→    data_area['auto_learn_trials'] = data_sess['auto_learn_trials'] # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64\n   456→    data_area['early_lick_trials'] = data_sess['early_lick_trials'] # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64\n   457→    data_area['auto_water_trials'] = data_sess['auto_water_trials'] # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64\n   458→    data_area['free_water_trials'] = data_sess['free_water_trials'] # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64\n   459→    data_area['lick_directions'] = data_sess['lick_directions'] # ndarray, (n_trials). Each element is a list of 0 (lick right) and 1 (lick left)\n   460→    data_area['lick_times'] = data_sess['lick_times'] # list with length n_trials, each element is 1D ndarray with all lick times in that trial\n   461→    data_area['gocue_time'] = data_sess['gocue_time']\n   462→    data_area['correctness'] = data_sess['correctness'] # ndarray, (n_trials,), int64. 1: correct or free water, 0: error, -1: no response\n   463→    data_area['neuron_info'] = data_sess['neuron_info'][neuron_id_area,:] # ndarray (n_units, 8)\n   464→\n   465→    data_area['ccf_coordinate'] = data_sess['ccf_coordinate'][neuron_id_area,:]  # ndarray (n_neurons,3), xyz coordinates\n   466→    data_area['ccf_label'] = list(np.array(data_sess['ccf_label'])[neuron_id_area]) # list of string, with whitespace at the beginning and end of each string removed\n   467→    data_area['ccf_unit_id'] = data_sess['ccf_unit_id'][neuron_id_area] # ndarray (n_neurons,), id of units with histology\n   468→\n   469→    data_area['probe_info'] = 'reorganized'\n   470→    data_area['delay_period'] = data_sess['delay_period'] # (n_trials,) duration of delay period\n   471→    data_area['sample_period'] = data_sess['sample_period'] # (n_trials,) duration of sample period\n   472→    data_area['stimulation'] = data_sess['stimulation'] # ndarray, (n_trials,4). The 4 columns are [laser_power, stim_type('1', '2', or '6', with 1,2,6 being left/right/both ALM perturbation), laser_on_time, laser_off_time]. For no stim trials, we have [0, NaN, NaN, NaN]\n   473→    data_area['trial_type'] = data_sess['trial_type'] # ndarray, (n_trials,). 1 is left, 0 is right 'r'\n   474→\n   475→    return data_area\n   476→\n   477→\n   478→def process_all_sess(bw, stride, begin_time, end_time, data_folder, save_folder, qc_mode):\n   479→    # scan through all sessions, and find files within the same session\n   480→    # all_path_list = sorted(list(glob.glob(os.path.join(data_folder, 'MAP_dandi', 'map*.mat')))) # SC065_20210507_172825_s8\n   481→    all_path_list = sorted(list(glob.glob(os.path.join(data_folder, 'MAP_dandi', 'map-export_SC050_20210302_140243_s20_p*.mat')))) # SC011_20190222_130111_s4\n   482→\n   483→    name2paths = OrderedDict() # keys are part of file names that are the same for all sessions. Empty 0x1 cell. Need to manually define what it becomes in Python.\n2. 1x1 cell. Will be directly read as non-iterables such as str, int, float, etc. Need to manually convert it to an iterable with length 1.\n3. Nx1 cell (N >= 2). Usually it's fine to read and directly store it in Python.\n\nComment: in case 2, when using isinstance(data, data_type), if data could be a number, consider the possibility of data being either float or int, i.e. use\n    if isinstance(data, float) or isinstance(data, int):\n        do something\n\"\"\"\n\nplt.rcParams['pdf.fonttype'] = 42\nplt.rcParams['ps.fonttype'] = 42\n\n\ndef sliding_histogram(spikeTimes, begin_time, end_time, bin_width, stride, rate=True):\n    '''\n    Calculates the number of spikes for each unit in each sliding bin of width bin_width, strided by stride.\n    begin_time and end_time are treated as the lower- and upper- bounds for bin centers.\n    if rate=True, calculate firing rates instead of bin spikes.\n    '''\n    bin_begin_time = begin_time\n    bin_end_time = end_time\n    # This is to deal with cases where for e.g. (bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride is actually 43 but evaluates to 42.999999... due to numerical errors in floating-point arithmetic.\n    if np.allclose((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride, math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+1):\n        n_bins =  math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+2\n    else:\n        n_bins = math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+1\n    binCenters = bin_begin_time + np.arange(n_bins)*stride\n\n    binIntervals = np.vstack((binCenters-bin_width/2, binCenters+bin_width/2)).T\n\n    # Count number of spikes for each sliding bin\n    binSpikes = np.asarray([[[np.sum(np.all([trial>=binInt[0], trial<binInt[1]], axis=0)) for binInt in binIntervals] for trial in unit]\n        for unit in spikeTimes]).swapaxes(0,-1)\n\n    if rate:\n        return binCenters, binSpikes/float(bin_width) # get firing rates\n    return binCenters, binSpikes\n\n\ndef process_one_sess(data_folder, file_path_list, bw, stride, begin_time=-3.0, end_time=3.5, save_folder=os.path.join(os.pardir, 'data_processed'), qc_mode='classifier'):\n    \"\"\"\n    Process all probe files in one session.\n\n    Args:\n        data_folder (str): the folder containing all data including QC neuron ID files.\n        file_path_list: list of full file paths of all probes in this session.\n        bw (float): in sec.\n        stride (float): in sec.\n        qc_mode (str): 'classifier' or 'old'.\n    \"\"\"\n    print(qc_mode)\n    if qc_mode == 'classifier':\n        qc_folder = os.path.join(data_folder, 'goodunits')\n        print(qc_folder)\n    elif qc_mode == 'old':\n        qc_folder = os.path.join(data_folder, 'DJ_GoodUnitsIdx_14regions_old_qc')\n    else:\n        raise ValueError('Wrong qc_mode! I'm wondering whether auto-water and free-water trials should be filtered out or treated similarly to the other behavioral variables. The NWB conversion might have different trial fields than the original .mat files. I'm realizing the distinction between the general preprocessing step, which keeps all trials, and the downstream analysis code that applies filtering like `get_regular_trial_mask`. The reference analysis does filter trials, so I need to check whether the AI should be doing that filtering or if it's correctly leaving it for later stages.\n\nFor the `time_from_tone_onset_s` calculation, the AI is pulling sample onset times from the behavioral events, finding the last sample before each go cue, then computing the time offset relative to the go cue. Since the sample happens before the go cue, this offset is negative, and adding it to the bin centers effectively gives the time elapsed since tone onset.\n\nRegarding the sample epoch itself—it consists of three tones played sequentially, so `sample_start_times` marks the beginning of that entire epoch. The AI uses `last_event_before_per_trial` to grab the most recent sample event before the go cue, which handles cases where early licks might trigger replays. This approach makes sense for aligning to the tone onset as described in the methods.\n\nComputing photostim alignment... The discretization scheme matches the reference implementation—session-wide percentiles determine the bins, and the tongue y-position is aligned using 50ms windows with the last sample in each bin, consistent with how the reference code handles frame alignment to time windows. The AI's approach clips indices to handle missing data, falling back to the last available sample if a bin has no data points, which it tracks with a fallback counter. The 50ms binning strategy aligns with the instructions, and now I'm thinking through which steps consume the most computation—particularly the unit iteration in the spike counting function. firing rates might be unnecessary if spike counts alone work for the decoder, and there's also the overhead of creating the sample dataset pickle file and loading all spike times including units that aren't marked as good. Before I finalize my analysis, let me verify the bin edge convention being used—it creates bins from -2.5 to 1.55 seconds with 80 total bins, where each bin interval is left-inclusive. The `is_good_trials` field marks which trials are valid for each unit based on spike sorting quality, but the reference implementation doesn't use it for trial-level filtering—only behavioral masks matter there. The tone onset time is computed relative to the go cue (`sample_rel = sample_start - go_time`, which is negative). From CONVERSION_NOTES.md: \"Input photostim_on: binary per bin, 1 if the photostim interval overlaps the bin.\" This matches the instructions: \"Whether photostimulation is on at every time point (discrete, time-varying).\"\n\n## 4-c. How is the `input` *Photostimulation* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Photostimulation timing is converted from trial-start-relative to go-cue-relative coordinates, then binned using the same bin edges as the neural data. This ensures temporal alignment.\n\nii.\n```python\nbin_left = BIN_EDGES_S[:-1][None, :]\nbin_right = BIN_EDGES_S[1:][None, :]\n# ...\nrel_on = (trial_start[trial_idx] + onset) - go_times[trial_idx]\nrel_off = rel_on + duration\noverlap = (bin_left < rel_off) & (bin_right > rel_on)\n```\n\niii. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Lick direction choice*?\n\ni. Three-tier choice determination:\n1. The photostim onset (relative to trial start) is converted to go-cue-relative time. A binary 0/1 value is assigned per bin: 1 if the photostim interval overlaps the bin, 0 otherwise. Trials without photostimulation (N/A onset or duration) get all zeros.\n\nii.\n```python\nonset = as_float_or_none(onset_values[trial_idx])\nduration = as_float_or_none(duration_values[trial_idx])\nif onset is None or duration is None:\n    continue\nrel_on = (trial_start[trial_idx] + onset) - go_times[trial_idx]\nrel_off = rel_on + duration\noverlap = (bin_left < rel_off) & (bin_right > rel_on)\nphotostim[trial_idx] = overlap.astype(np.float32)[0]\n```\n\niii. From CONVERSION_NOTES.md: \"Input photostim_on: binary per bin, 1 if the photostim interval overlaps the bin.\" This matches the instructions: \"Whether photostimulation is on at every time point (discrete, time-varying).\"\n\n## 4-c. How is the `input` *Photostimulation* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Photostimulation timing is converted from trial-start-relative to go-cue-relative coordinates, then binned using the same bin edges as the neural data. This ensures temporal alignment.\n\nii.\n```python\nbin_left = BIN_EDGES_S[:-1][None, :]\nbin_right = BIN_EDGES_S[1:][None, :]\n# ...\nrel_on = (trial_start[trial_idx] + onset) - go_times[trial_idx]\nrel_off = rel_on + duration\noverlap = (bin_left < rel_off) & (bin_right > rel_on)\n```\n\niii. The same `BIN_EDGES_S` array is used for both neural binning and photostim binning, ensuring alignment.\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Lick direction choice* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from `BehavioralEvents/left_lick_times/timestamps` and `BehavioralEvents/right_lick_times/timestamps` (actual lick event times), with `intervals/trials/trial_instruction` as a fallback for trials with no licks.\n\nii.\n```python\nleft_lick_times = np.asarray(behavioral_events[\"left_lick_times\"][\"timestamps\"][:], dtype=np.float64)\nright_lick_times = np.asarray(behavioral_events[\"right_lick_times\"][\"timestamps\"][:], dtype=np.float64)\ninstructions = decode_array(trial_group[\"trial_instruction\"][:])[trial_indices].astype(str)\n```\n\niii. The AI uses actual lick behavior to determine choice rather than the instructed trial type. This is appropriate for a \"choice\" variable that reflects the animal's decision.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Lick direction choice*?\n\ni. Three-tier choice determination:\n1. First post-go-cue lick: if licks occur after the go cue, the side of the first lick determines choice.\n2. First any-trial lick: if no post-go licks but there are licks elsewhere in the trial, the first lick's side is used.\n3. Instruction fallback: if no licks at all, the instructed trial side is used.\n\nLeft = 0, right = 1.\n\nii.\n```python\nleft_post = left_all[left_all >= go_time]\nright_post = right_all[right_all >= go_time]\n\nif left_post.size or right_post.size:\n    left_first = left_post[0] if left_post.size else np.inf\n    right_first = right_post[0] if right_post.size else np.inf\n    choice[trial_idx] = 0 if left_first < right_first else 1\n    source_counter[\"post_go_lick\"] += 1\n    continue\n\nif left_all.size or right_all.size:\n    # ... fallback to any trial lick\n    continue\n\nchoice[trial_idx] = 0 if instructions[trial_idx] == \"left\" else 1\nsource_counter[\"instruction_fallback\"] += 1\n```\n\niii. From CONVERSION_NOTES.md: \"79,585 trials using the preferred first post-go lick rule, 1,063 trials using first-lick-anywhere fallback, and 12,662 no-lick trials requiring instructed-side fallback.\"\n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Outcome* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from `intervals/trials/outcome` in the NWB trials table, which contains string values: \"hit\", \"miss\", or \"ignore\".\n\nii.\n```python\noutcomes_raw = decode_array(trial_group[\"outcome\"][:])[trial_indices].astype(str)\noutcome_map = {\"ignore\": 0, \"miss\": 1, \"hit\": 2}\noutcome = np.array([outcome_map[x] for x in outcomes_raw], dtype=np.int8)\n```\n\niii. The instructions specify: \"Outcome (ignore = 0, miss = 1, hit = 2, per-trial).\"\n\n## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Outcome*?\n\ni. Direct string-to-integer mapping: \"ignore\" -> 0, \"miss\" -> 1, \"hit\" -> 2. The outcome is a per-trial categorical value broadcast across all time bins.\n\nii.\n```python\noutcome_map = {\"ignore\": 0, \"miss\": 1, \"hit\": 2}\noutcome = np.array([outcome_map[x] for x in outcomes_raw], dtype=np.int8)\n# In output construction:\nnp.full(len(BIN_CENTERS_S), outcome[trial_idx], dtype=np.int8),\n```\n\niii. Straightforward mapping matching the instructions. The value is constant across time bins within a trial.\n\n## 6-c. How is `output` *Distance to reward zone* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. There is no \"Distance to reward zone\" output in this task. The outcome variable is a per-trial scalar (not time-varying) that is broadcast to all time bins. No temporal alignment is needed beyond assigning the trial's outcome to each bin.\n\nii.\n```python\nnp.full(len(BIN_CENTERS_S), outcome[trial_idx], dtype=np.int8),\n```\n\niii. The outcome is constant within each trial, so alignment is trivial - each time bin gets the same value.\n\n## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Early lick* derived from?\n\ni. The reference code uses `camera_0_side` -> `tongue_y` from the .mat files; the NWB equivalent is the TongueTracking timeseries, column 1.\n\n## 8-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Tongue y-position*?\n\ni. Session-wide 40th and 60th percentiles are computed over all tongue y-position samples in the session (not just within-trial data). These percentiles define the discretization thresholds.\n\nii.\n```python\nq40 = float(np.percentile(tongue_y_raw, 40))\nq60 = float(np.percentile(tongue_y_raw, 60))\n```\n\niii. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Several missing-data scenarios are handled:\n- **Sessions with zero good units**: Skipped entirely (1 session excluded).\n- **No-lick trials**: Choice falls back to instruction side for trials with no lick events.\n- **Missing tongue frames**: If a 50ms bin has no tongue tracking frames, the last available frame before the bin is used (`np.clip(right_idx - 1, 0, ...)`).\n- **Missing photostim fields**: Trials where onset or duration is \"N/A\" or empty get all-zero photostim.\n- **obs_intervals mismatch**: Errors are raised if ephys trials cannot be matched to behavioral trials.\n\nii.\n```python\n# Missing photostim:\nif onset is None or duration is None:\n    continue  # leaves zeros\n\n# No-lick fallback:\nchoice[trial_idx] = 0 if instructions[trial_idx] == \"left\" else 1\n\n# Missing tongue frames:\nsample_idx = np.clip(right_idx - 1, 0, len(timestamps) - 1)\n\n# Zero good units:\nif n_good_units == 0:\n    return None\n```\n\niii. The two most time-consuming steps are:\n1. **`bin_spike_counts_for_good_units`**: Iterates over ALL units (including non-good) to find good ones, then processes each good unit's spikes with numpy operations. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. Three main loops could be vectorized:\n1. **`compute_choice_labels`**: Per-trial Python loop (lines 100-127) searching for licks in each trial. Could use vectorized `searchsorted` and array operations.\n2. **`bin_photostim_series`**: Per-trial Python loop (lines 144-153) computing bin overlaps. Already has vectorized bin overlap logic but wraps it in a per-trial loop.\n3. **`bin_spike_counts_for_good_units`**: Per-unit loop (lines 197-222). The inner operations are vectorized (numpy), but the outer unit loop is Python.\n\nii.\n```python\n# compute_choice_labels - per-trial loop:\nfor trial_idx in range(len(trial_start)):\n    left_all = left_lick_times[(left_lick_times >= start) & (left_lick_times <= stop)]\n    # ...\n\n# bin_photostim_series - per-trial loop:\nfor trial_idx in range(n_trials):\n    onset = as_float_or_none(onset_values[trial_idx])\n    # ...\n```\n\niii. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n\ni.\n1. **`build_dataset` called twice**: Once for the full dataset and once for the sample dataset. The sample reuses already-computed session results, so only the aggregation is repeated (lightweight).\n2. **Reading all spike times**: The code reads `units/spike_times[:]` (all units) but only processes good units. The non-good units' spike times are loaded but discarded.\n3. **Decoding byte strings**: `decode_array` is called multiple times on different NWB fields, each iterating through all values.\n\nii.\n```python\n# build_dataset called twice:\nfull_data, full_summary = build_dataset(session_results)\nsample_data, sample_summary = build_dataset(sample_results)\n```\n\niii. The double `build_dataset` call is intentional (sample vs. full), and the overhead is minimal since session conversion (the expensive part) is not repeated.\n\n## 10-d. What unnecessary processing does the code do that is discarded in downstream analyses?\n\ni.\n1. **Firing rate conversion**: Spike counts are divided by bin_size to produce firing rates (`* (1.0 / BIN_SIZE_S)`). Many decoders work with spike counts directly; this division is a linear scaling that doesn't affect decoding.\n2. **Loading all units' spike times**: The entire `units/spike_times` array is read, but only good units are used. Could read only good units' spike time slices.\n3. **Sample data creation**: The `sample_data.pkl` file duplicates a subset of the full dataset. If only the full dataset is needed, this processing is wasted.\n4. **float16 precision for neural data**: Converting to float16 (`spike_counts[trial_idx].T.astype(np.float16)`) may discard precision unnecessarily.\n\nii.\n```python\n# Firing rate conversion (linear scaling):\nneural_trials.append((spike_counts[trial_idx].T.astype(np.float16) * (1.0 / BIN_SIZE_S)))\n\n# Loading all spike times including non-good units:\nspike_times_flat=np.asarray(f[\"units/spike_times\"][:], dtype=np.float64),\n```\n\niii. The firing rate conversion preserves interpretability (spikes/s vs raw counts) but doesn't affect decoder performance. as_float_or_none(duration_values[trial_idx])\n        if onset is None or duration is None:\n            continue\n        stim_trial_count += 1\n        rel_on = (trial_start[trial_idx] + onset) - go_times[trial_idx]\n        rel_off = rel_on + duration\n        overlap = (bin_left < rel_off) & (bin_right > rel_on)\n        photostim[trial_idx] = overlap.astype(np.float32)[0]\n\n    return {"type":"item.completed","item":{"id":"item_13","type":"command_execution","command":"/bin/bash -lc \"sed -n '1,120p' /app/methods.txt\"","aggregated_output":"Behavior and video tracking\n\nMice performed an auditory delayed response task 21 (Figure 1A). The instruction stimuli during the sample epoch were pure tones played at one of two frequencies (3 kHz or 12 kHz). Each tone was played three times for 150 ms with 100 ms inter-tone intervals. The sample epoch was followed by a 1.2 s delay epoch. An auditory ‘Go’ cue (carrier frequency of 6 kHz with 360 Hz modulating fre-\nquency, 0.1 s duration) indicated the end of the delay epoch. Licking early during the sample/delay epoch triggered a replay of the epoch. During the response epoch (answer period: 1.5 s) mice reported the instruction by licking one of the two lick ports. Licking (consumption period: 1.5 s) the correct lick port triggered a small water reward (0.1 ~ 0.2 ul). Licking the incorrect lick port triggered a timeout (1-3 s). After mice stopped licking for 1.5 s, the trial ended, followed by a 250 ms inter-trial-interval. Early lick trials and no\nresponse trials were excluded for analysis. Overall performance was computed as the fraction of correct control trials (i.e. no photostimulation), excluding any early lick trials. We selected experimental sessions for analysis based on following criteria: overall behavioral performance (> 65%), and at least 50 correct lick left and lick right trials each. Mice performed on average 476 (Mean; range, 130-785) trials per session with 84% correct rate (range, 65-99%; STAR Methods). During the delay epoch, mice maintain a memory of their choice and plan a movement.\n\nTwo CMOS cameras (CM3-U3-13Y3M, FLIR) were used to track orofacial movements of the mouse under IR light illumination (940 nm LED). The cameras were equipped with 4-12 mm focal length lenses (12VM412ASIR, Tamron) and a pixel resolution of 71 mm. High-speed videos from a side view and a bottom view (Figure 1C) were acquired at 300 Hz using software FlyCapture (TELEDYNE FLIR). We trained DeepLabCut 39 to track the movement of tongue, jaw and nose (Figure 1I).\n\nMulti-regional electrophysiological recordings\n\nWe designed a ﬂexible manipulator system for multiple Neuropixels probe insertions (https://www.janelia.org/open-science/manipulator-system-for-multiple-neuropixels-probe-recordings). Three to four small craniotomies (diameter, 1 \u0018 1.5 mm) were made over target brain areas one day before the ﬁrst recording session. Details of recordings made with multiple Neuropixels probes in head-restrained behaving animals have been published (dx.doi.org/10.17504/protocols.io.8tphwmn). Target regions, insertions angles, probe types, etc. are summarized in Table S2. All coordinates are given with respect to Bregma (AP, anterior-posterior; ML, medial-lateral; DV, dorsal-ventral).\n\nExtracellular spikes were recorded using Neuropixels 1.0 probes, 2.0 single-shank (SS) and 2.0 multi-shank (MS) probes 3,4 (Table S2). Three to seven recordings were made from each craniotomy. Recording depth was inferred from manipulator readings (Sensapax uMp manipulators). After completion of probe insertion, brain tissue was allowed to settle for several minutes before recording. During recordings the craniotomies were immersed in saline.\n\nProbes were conﬁgured and data were visualized and streamed to disk using the open-source software package SpikeGLX (https://billkarsh.github.io/SpikeGLX/). Data from up to ﬁve simultaneously recorded probes were acquired at 30 kHz. Sync waves (0.5 s duty cycle TTL pulses) were recorded across probes and on auxiliary channels for synchronization.\n\nPhotoinhibition\n\nLight from two 473 nm lasers (Coherent OBIS LS/LX) was used to illuminate the two hemispheres of ALM. The laser power was controlled by analog outputs controlled by a Bpod (Sanworks). Photoinactivation of ALM (centered on AP 2.5 mm; ML 1.5 mm) was performed through clear-skull cap implant11 or via craniotomy by directing the laser over the skull (beam diameter, 1.8 mm at 4 sigma). Photoinhibition of the left-, right-ALM hemisphere, or both hemispheres was deployed on a subset of ~25% randomly interleaved trials 11,21 (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice). To prevent the mice from distinguishing photostimulation trials from control rials using visual cues, a ‘masking ﬂash’ (1 ms pulses at 10 Hz) was delivered using 470 nm LEDs in front of the mice throughout the entire trial. For silencing ALM, we stimulated cortical GABAergic neurons in VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice. We used 40 Hz photostimulation with a sinusoidal temporal proﬁle (5 mW average power per hemisphere) and a 100 ms linear ramping down during the laser offset to reduce rebound neuronal activity. This photo stimulus is expected to silence glutamatergic neurons in all of ALM. 37 We silenced ALM activity during the late delay epoch (last 0.5 s), including the 100 ms ramp-down. Thus, photoinhibition always ended before the ‘Go’ cue.\n\nTo analyze the effects of photoinhibition, units with at least 10 photostimulation trials were selected for analysis (Figures 5 and S5). Bilateral ALM photostimulation reduced behavior performance from 83.2% to 71.7% (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice, n = 93 sessions). To exclude the effects of rebound population activity in the thalamus 16 and other subcortical regions, we used a 20-120 ms\ntime window after photo stimulation onset to measure the effect of ALM inactivation in downstream regions (Figures 5 and S5). For ALM itself, we used 500 ms (i.e. the entire inactivation period) to calculate spike rate reduction.\n\nSpike sorting and quality control\n\nWe developed a data pipeline for preprocessing, spike sorting and quality control (https://github.com/jenniferColonell/ecephys_spike_sorting). The extracellular recording traces were ﬁrst band-pass ﬁltered (Butterworth acausal ﬁlter, 250 Hz - 9 kHz). We then sorted the dataset using both Kilosort2 32 (MATLAB version, GitHub - jenniferColonell/KS20_for_preprocessed_data: Release version of KS2 for preprocessed data) and Kilosort2.5 4 (Python version, GitHub - jenniferColonell/pykilosort: [WIP] Python port of Kilosort 2). Both sorting methods gave similar results. In the analyses presented in the paper, we used the output from Kilosort2. Each output cluster was associated with a list of 15 quality metrics calculated to assess isolation and sorting quality 7 (i.e. quality control) (https://github.com/AllenInstitute/ecephys_spike_sorting/tree/master/ecephys_spike_sorting/modules/quality_metrics).\n\nThe details of the spike sorting and quality control are described in an accompanying white paper (https://doi.org/10.25378/janelia.24066108.v1). In brief, we used 15 cluster quality metrics to train classiﬁers - one for each of the major ﬁve brain areas (cortex, striatum, thalamus, midbrain and medulla). The classiﬁers were trained based on labels derived from manual curation. We curated Kilosort2 output (28 penetrations from ﬁve brain areas) using the open-source graphical user interface Phy (GitHub - cortex-lab/phy: phy: interactive visualization and manual spike sorting of large-scale ephys data). Each cluster was labeled as a ‘good’ or ‘unlabeled’ unit. Annotators were blind to location, recording condition and trial type information. Based on the annotated labels of all clusters and associated quality metrics, we trained ﬁve region-speciﬁc classiﬁers using logistic regression. Applying the trained classiﬁers to the rest of the dataset provided lists of units that were labeled as ‘good’ and were used for the analysis in this paper. We applied the cortex classiﬁer to ALM and other cortex, hippocampus, olfactory and cortical subplate units; the striatum classiﬁer to striatum and pallidum units; the thalamus classiﬁer to thalamus and hypothalamus units; the midbrain classiﬁer to midbrain and pons units; the medulla classiﬁer to medulla and cerebellum units. We calculated the rate at which the classiﬁer incorrectly labeled ’unlabeled’ units as ’good’, using 10-fold cross-validation on held-out manually labeled units (false alarm rate): 7.8%; striatum: 6.4%; thalamus: 7.3%; midbrain: 5.5%; medulla 4.3%. This method yielded 8717 good units from ALM, 7664 from striatum, 12808 from thalamus, 7495 from midbrain and 2928 from medulla, etc. Overall, the dataset consisted of 69,943 good units recorded across 173 behavioral sessions, from which 655 probe insertions were made. This corresponds to 25.9 % of clusters reported by Kilosort2. \n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

Usually it's fine to read and directly store it in Python.\n    26\t\n    27\tComment: in case 2, when using isinstance(data, data_type), if data could be a number, consider the possibility of data being either float or int, i.e. use\n    28\t    if isinstance(data, float) or isinstance(data, int):\n    29\t        do something\n    30\t\"\"\"\n    31\t\n    32\tplt.rcParams['pdf.fonttype'] = 42\n    33\tplt.rcParams['ps.fonttype'] = 42\n    34\t\n    35\t\n    36\tdef sliding_histogram(spikeTimes, begin_time, end_time, bin_width, stride, rate=True):\n    37\t    '''\n    38\t    Calculates the number of spikes for each unit in each sliding bin of width bin_width, strided by stride.\n    39\t    begin_time and end_time are treated as the lower- and upper- bounds for bin centers.\n    40\t    if rate=True, calculate firing rates instead of bin spikes.\n    41\t    '''\n    42\t    bin_begin_time = begin_time\n    43\t    bin_end_time = end_time\n    44\t    # This is to deal with cases where for e.g. (bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride is actually 43 but evaluates to 42.999999... due to numerical errors in floating-point arithmetic.\n    45\t    if np.allclose((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride, math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+1):\n    46\t        n_bins =  math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+2\n    47\t    else:\n    48\t        n_bins = math.floor((bin_end_time-bin_begin_time)/stride)+1\n    49\t    binCenters = bin_begin_time + np.arange(n_bins)*stride\n    50\t\n    51\t    binIntervals = np.vstack((binCenters-bin_width/2, binCenters+bin_width/2)).T\n    52\t\n    53\t    # Count number of spikes for each sliding bin\n    54\t    binSpikes = np.asarray([[[np.sum(np.all([trial>=binInt[0], trial<binInt[1]], axis=0)) for binInt in binIntervals] for trial in unit]\n    55\t        for unit in spikeTimes]).swapaxes(0,-1)\n    56\t\n    57\t    if rate:\n    58\t        return binCenters, binSpikes/float(bin_width) # get firing rates\n    59\t    return binCenters, binSpikes\n    60\t\n    61\t\n    62\tdef process_one_sess(data_folder, file_path_list, bw, stride, begin_time=-3.0, end_time=3.5, save_folder=os.path.join(os.pardir, 'data_processed'), qc_mode='classifier'):\n    63\t    \"\"\"\n    64\t    Process all probe files in one session.\n    65\t\n    66\t    Args:\n    67\t        data_folder (str): the folder containing all data including QC neuron ID files.\n    68\t        file_path_list: list of full file paths of all probes in this session.\n    69\t        bw (float): in sec.\n    70\t        stride (float): in sec.\n    71\t        qc_mode (str): 'classifier' or 'old'.\n    72\t    \"\"\"\n    73\t    print(qc_mode)\n    74\t    if qc_mode == 'classifier':\n    75\t        qc_folder = os.path.join(data_folder, 'goodunits')\n    76\t        print(qc_folder)\n    77\t    elif qc_mode == 'old':\n    78\t        qc_folder = os.path.join(data_folder, 'DJ_GoodUnitsIdx_14regions_old_qc')\n    79\t    else:\n    80\t        raise ValueError('Wrong qc_mode!
